<a id="eq:family-jacobian"></a>
Differentiating $N'$ and then $Y_{v,b}$, with $v,b$ fixed, gives $$dN'=D^{-1}\bigl(B\,dN+S\,dg\,N'\bigr)=D^{-1}O\,dN,$$ and consequently $$\label{eq:family-jacobian}
 \begin{split}
 dY_{v,b}
 &=rO\,dx+(b+rSf)D^{-1}O\,dN
   =O(rI-h_{v,b}A),\\
 J_mY_{v,b}&=\bigl|\det(rI-h_{v,b}A)\bigr|.
 \end{split}$$ Here $J_m$ is the metric $m$-dimensional Jacobian; the isometry $O$ accounts for the entire ambient change in the differential.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Every level has the required perimeter bound

Define $$E_{v,b}=\{y\in S^{m+1}:\psi_v(y)<b\},\qquad
 \Lambda_{v,b}=\{y\in S^{m+1}:\psi_v(y)=b\}.$$ For finite boosts and interior levels, also define the closed source set $$T_{v,b}=\{x\in\Sigma:rI-h_{v,b}(x)A(x)\ge0\}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-family-all-levels"></a>
<div id="lem:family-all-levels" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:family-restricted-perimeter"></a>
<a id="eq:family-restricted-density"></a>
**Lemma 21** (The restricted area bound). *Every $E_{v,b}$, for $(v,b)\in Q$, has finite perimeter. For $|v|<1$, $|b|<1$ and every open set $U\subset S^{m+1}$, $$\label{eq:family-restricted-perimeter}
 \begin{split}
 \operatorname{Per}(E_{v,b};U)
 &\le\mathcal H^m(\Lambda_{v,b}\cap U)\\
 &\le\int_{T_{v,b}\cap Y_{v,b}^{-1}(U)}
       \det(rI-h_{v,b}A)\,d\mu,
 \end{split}$$ and on $T_{v,b}$, $$\label{eq:family-restricted-density}
 0\le\det(rI-h_{v,b}A)\le r^m\le1.$$ Thus these estimates hold at every level, without an exceptional set of values of $b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-family-all-levels"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-family-all-levels</summary>

*Proof.* Take $y\in\Lambda_{v,b}$ and transform $(y,b)$ to $(z,h)$ by <a href="#eq:family-boost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-boost">[eq:family-boost]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:family-cone-contact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-cone-contact">19</a> supplies a closest point $r_zx$. Lorentz invariance gives $r_z^2=|z|^2-h^2=1-b^2$, so $r_z=r$. The inverse formulas then show that $h=h_{v,b}(x)$, $y=Y_{v,b}(x)$, and $x\in T_{v,b}$. Therefore <a id="eq:family-level-cover"></a>$$\label{eq:family-level-cover}
 \Lambda_{v,b}\subset Y_{v,b}(T_{v,b}).$$ The nonnegative eigenvalues of $rI-h_{v,b}A$ have sum $mr$, because $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A=0$. Their arithmetic–geometric mean inequality proves <a href="#eq:family-restricted-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-restricted-density">[eq:family-restricted-density]</a>. The area formula (Federer 1969, vol. 153, sec. 3.2.3) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[Federer1969](#ref-Federer1969)] <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[Federer1978](#ref-Federer1978)] (Federer 1978, sec. 3, pp. 299–300), with multiplicities counted in its source integral, now yields the second inequality in <a href="#eq:family-restricted-perimeter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-restricted-perimeter">[eq:family-restricted-perimeter]</a>. The condition defining $T_{v,b}$ is only necessary for a closest-point contact. Additional points in this source set, and multiple preimages of a level point, increase the upper bound and cause no difficulty.

In particular $\mathcal H^m(\Lambda_{v,b})<\infty$. Since $E_{v,b}$ is open, its essential boundary is contained in its topological boundary, which is contained in $\Lambda_{v,b}$. The finite-essential-boundary criterion (Federer 1978, sec. 4, p. 302) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[Federer1978](#ref-Federer1978)], applied in coordinate charts, gives finite perimeter. The intrinsic reduced-boundary representation (Bögelein, Duzaar, and Fusco 2017, sec. 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[BDFSphere](#ref-BDFSphere)] then gives $$\operatorname{Per}(E_{v,b};U)
 =\mathcal H^m(\partial^*E_{v,b}\cap U)
 \le\mathcal H^m(\Lambda_{v,b}\cap U).$$ This proves the first inequality with constant one.

For $|v|<1$ the endpoint sublevels are empty and full, respectively. For $|v|=1$, formula <a href="#eq:family-boundary-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-boundary-graph">[eq:family-boundary-graph]</a> makes them round caps, whose boundary masses are $\sigma_m(1-b^2)^{m/2}$, with zero mass at $b=\pm1$. These cases also have finite perimeter. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:family-filling-continuity"></a>
All of the levels $\Lambda_{v,b}$ have zero $(m+1)$-dimensional volume: this follows from their finite $m$-dimensional measure in the interior; on the boost boundary they are round spheres or singletons; and at finite-boost endpoints they are empty. If $(v_j,b_j)\to(v,b)$, Lemma <a href="#lem:family-graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-graph">20</a> gives uniform convergence of $\psi_{v_j}-b_j$ to $\psi_v-b$. Indicators of the sublevels consequently converge pointwise off $\Lambda_{v,b}$. Dominated convergence proves $$\label{eq:family-filling-continuity}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}_{m+1}(E_{v_j,b_j}\mathbin\triangle E_{v,b})\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $$\widehat\Phi(v,b)=\partial[E_{v,b}]$$ is flat continuous on $Q$, since the flat distance between two of these cycles is at most the volume of the symmetric difference of their fillings. Its mass equals the perimeter of the filling, so <a href="#eq:family-mass-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-mass-bounds">[eq:family-mass-bounds]</a> follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:family-all-levels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-all-levels">21</a>.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Infinite boosts and no concentration of mass

We have obtained a flat-continuous family with the required total mass bound. To apply the min–max theorem, we must also control its mass in small balls uniformly over parameters, including boosts tending to infinity. The restricted Jacobian estimate lets us do this on the one fixed compact source $\Sigma$.

<a id="stmt-lem-family-no-concentration"></a>
<div id="lem:family-no-concentration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:family-no-concentration"></a>
**Lemma 22** (No concentration). *The family $\widehat\Phi$ satisfies $$\label{eq:family-no-concentration}
 \lim_{\rho\downarrow0}\;
 \sup_{(v,b)\in Q}\sup_{y\in S^{m+1}}
 \|\widehat\Phi(v,b)\|(B_\rho(y))=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-family-no-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-family-no-concentration</summary>

*Proof.* We prove the sequential form of the assertion. Consider $u_j=(v_j,b_j)\to(v_*,b_*)$, $y_j\to y_*$, and $\rho_j\downarrow0$. The case $|b_*|=1$ follows directly from <a href="#eq:family-mass-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-mass-bounds">[eq:family-mass-bounds]</a>. Parameters on $|v|=1$ are harmless as well: an $m$-sphere of Euclidean radius $r$ in any affine $(m+1)$-plane has at most $c_m\rho^m$ area in a ball of radius $\rho$, uniformly in $r$ and the plane. For $r\le2\rho$ use its total area; for $r>2\rho$ the intersection lies in a spherical cap with angular radius at most $\arcsin(\rho/r)$, and the same bound follows by the cap area formula. Spherical balls in the ambient unit sphere lie in Euclidean balls of the same radius. We may therefore pass to a subsequence with $|v_j|<1$ and $|b_*|<1$.

<a id="eq:family-concentration-integrands"></a>
Put $Y_j=Y_{v_j,b_j}$ and $T_j=T_{v_j,b_j}$. The local perimeter bound reduces the desired assertion to $$\label{eq:family-concentration-integrands}
 \int_\Sigma I_j\,d\mu\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 I_j=\mathbf1_{T_j}\,
      \mathbf1_{Y_j^{-1}(B_{\rho_j}(y_j))}\,J_mY_j.$$ The restricted bound gives $0\le I_j\le1$ on the fixed compact source.

If $|v_*|<1$, the maps $Y_j$ converge smoothly on $\Sigma$ to $Y_*=Y_{v_*,b_*}$. Away from the fiber $Y_*^{-1}(y_*)$, the ball indicator in <a href="#eq:family-concentration-integrands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-concentration-integrands">[eq:family-concentration-integrands]</a> eventually vanishes. The area formula applied to the fiber gives $$\int_{Y_*^{-1}(y_*)}J_mY_*\,d\mu=0,$$ because a singleton has zero $\mathcal H^m$ measure. Thus $J_mY_*=0$ almost everywhere on that fiber, and smooth convergence gives $I_j\to0$ there almost everywhere as well. Dominated convergence completes this case.

It remains to consider $v_*=a$ with $|a|=1$. Write $a_j=v_j/|v_j|$, $t_j=S_j/C_j=|v_j|$, and $f_j=\langle a_j,x\rangle$, $g_j=\langle a_j,N\rangle$. The cancellation identity <a id="eq:family-cancelled-map"></a>$$\label{eq:family-cancelled-map}
 Bx+SfN'=x+f\,\frac{SN-(C-1)a}{D}$$ gives $$Y_j=r_j\left[x+
 f_j\frac{t_jN-(1-C_j^{-1})a_j}{1-t_jg_j}\right]+b_jN'_j.$$ On compact subsets of $\Sigma\setminus Z_a$, where $Z_a=\{x:N(x)=a\}$, the denominators stay uniformly positive. Also $$N'_j=
 \frac{C_j^{-1}N+(1-C_j^{-1})g_ja_j-t_ja_j}{1-t_jg_j}
 \longrightarrow-a.$$ The maps and all their source derivatives therefore converge locally to <a id="eq:family-infinite-map"></a>$$\label{eq:family-infinite-map}
 Y_\infty(x)=r_*\left[x+
       \frac{\langle a,x\rangle}{1-\langle a,N\rangle}(N-a)
                 \right]-b_*a,
 \qquad r_*=\sqrt{1-b_*^2}.$$ This computation allows the directions $a_j$ to vary.

The exceptional normal fiber $Z_a$ is source-null. Indeed $Z_a\subset\{x:\langle a,x\rangle=0\}$, whereas $\langle a,x\rangle$ is a nontrivial analytic function by Lemma <a href="#lem:fullness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fullness">5</a>. Its zero set has zero $m$-dimensional measure. No rank assumption on the Gauss map is involved.

The point fiber $F=Y_\infty^{-1}(y_*)$, defined off $Z_a$, may instead have positive source measure. On a countable cover by relatively compact coordinate patches in $\Sigma\setminus Z_a$, the area formula gives $$\int_{F\cap K}J_mY_\infty\,d\mu=0$$ for each such patch $K$. Hence $J_mY_\infty=0$ almost everywhere on $F$. Off $F\cup Z_a$ the shrinking-ball indicator is eventually zero; on $F$, local smooth convergence gives $J_mY_j\to0$ almost everywhere. We conclude that $I_j\to0$ almost everywhere on $\Sigma$, and $0\le I_j\le1$ again permits dominated convergence. In particular, convergence of the indicators $\mathbf1_{T_j}$ is unnecessary. Only the restricted Jacobians are bounded; no bound for the unrestricted $J_mY_j$ near $Z_a$ has been used.

Finally, if <a href="#eq:family-no-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-no-concentration">[eq:family-no-concentration]</a> failed, compactness of $Q$ and $S^{m+1}$ would supply sequences of the above form whose ball masses stay bounded below by a positive constant. The sequential result excludes them. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The projective parameter space

On $|v|=1$, formula <a href="#eq:family-boundary-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-boundary-graph">[eq:family-boundary-graph]</a> gives $$E_{v,b}=\{\langle v,y\rangle>-b\},\qquad
 E_{-v,-b}=\{\langle v,y\rangle<-b\}.$$ These fillings are complementary modulo their null common level. On the top and bottom faces they are full and empty, respectively. Together with the volume continuity in <a href="#eq:family-filling-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-filling-continuity">[eq:family-filling-continuity]</a>, these facts verify every hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#lem:minmax-projective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:minmax-projective">13</a>. Therefore $\widehat\Phi(u)=\partial[E_u]$ descends to a flat-continuous detecting map $\Phi$ on $\mathbb {RP}^{p}$. Its image is unchanged by passage to the quotient, so Lemma <a href="#lem:family-no-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-no-concentration">22</a> also gives no concentration for $\Phi$.

<a id="eq:family-gap"></a>
At $(0,0)$, $\psi_0=\varphi$ on the sphere, and the level is $\Sigma$, with sublevel $\Omega$. Combining the upper mass bound, Lemma <a href="#lem:family-no-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-no-concentration">22</a>, and Lemma <a href="#lem:detection-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detection-gap">14</a> proves part <span class="upright">(i)</span> of Proposition <a href="#prop:lorentz-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lorentz-family">18</a>, in particular $$\label{eq:family-gap}
 |\Sigma|\ge\gamma_m>\sigma_m.$$

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Uniform area loss away from the center

The family now detects the required cohomology class and has no concentration. We next prove that all parameters away from the original hypersurface lose a uniform amount of area. This isolates the maximum that the extra negative direction will remove.

<a id="stmt-lem-family-strict-loss"></a>
<div id="lem:family-strict-loss" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (A fixed source patch gives strict loss). *For every neighborhood $U$ of $(0,0)$, <a href="#eq:family-away-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-away-gap">[eq:family-away-gap]</a> holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-family-strict-loss"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-family-strict-loss</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose otherwise. There are $u_j=(v_j,b_j)\in Q\setminus U$ with $\mathbf M(\widehat\Phi(u_j))\to|\Sigma|$. The mass bound forces $b_j\to0$. Parameters on the boost boundary have mass at most $\sigma_m$, which is strictly smaller by <a href="#eq:family-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-gap">[eq:family-gap]</a>; discard those terms. After taking a subsequence, $v_j\to v_*\ne0$. Set $t_*=|v_*|\in(0,1]$ and $a=v_*/|v_*|$. Discard finitely many terms so that $v_j\ne0$. For each $j$, set $t_j=|v_j|$, $a_j=v_j/|v_j|$, $C_j=(1-t_j^2)^{-1/2}$, $r_j=\sqrt{1-b_j^2}$, $f_j=\langle a_j,x\rangle$, and $g_j=\langle a_j,N\rangle$.

The open set $\{A\ne0\}$ is nonempty. The analytic function $f=\langle a,x\rangle$ cannot vanish on that entire open set, by fullness and connectedness. Choose a compact source patch $K_0$ of positive measure on which $|A|$ and $|f|$ are bounded below by positive constants. It is also disjoint from $\{N=a\}$, because $N=a$ implies $f=0$. On this fixed patch, the formula for $h_j=h_{v_j,b_j}$ becomes $$h_j=\frac{b_j/C_j+t_jr_jf_j}{1-t_jg_j}
 \longrightarrow
 h_*:=\frac{t_*f}{1-t_*\langle a,N\rangle},$$ uniformly. Its limit is finite and nonzero on $K_0$, including when $t_*=1$.

For a real symmetric matrix $M$, define its truncated determinant by $$\mathcal D(M)=
 \begin{cases}
  \det M,&M\ge0,\\
  0,&M\not\ge0.
 \end{cases}$$ This is continuous: every boundary point of the positive-semidefinite cone is singular, so its determinant vanishes. At every $x\in K_0$, the matrix $I-h_*(x)A(x)$ has trace $m$ and is not the identity. If it is positive semidefinite, strictness in the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives determinant less than one; if it is not, $\mathcal D$ is zero. Compactness of $K_0$ and continuity therefore give $\delta>0$ with $$\mathcal D(I-h_*A)\le1-2\delta\quad\hbox{on }K_0.$$ For all large $j$ this yields $\mathcal D(r_jI-h_jA)\le r_j^m-\delta$ on $K_0$. On all of $\Sigma$, the restricted determinant is at most $r_j^m$. Thus the area formula estimate gives $$\mathbf M(\widehat\Phi(u_j))
 \le\int_\Sigma\mathcal D(r_jI-h_jA)\,d\mu
 \le r_j^m|\Sigma|-\delta\mu(K_0),$$ contradicting convergence to $|\Sigma|$. The proof supplies a uniform loss on a fixed portion of the source; it makes no upper-semicontinuity assumption about area in the flat topology. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The entire central level is a smooth boundary

<a id="stmt-lem-family-central-tube"></a>
<div id="lem:family-central-tube" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:family-regular-level"></a>
**Lemma 24** (The central tube). *For all sufficiently small $(v,b)$, the entire level $\Lambda_{v,b}$ is $Y_{v,b}(\Sigma)$, with $Y_{v,b}$ a smooth embedding depending smoothly on the parameters. Every level point has a unique closest-point contact in the cone representation. Moreover $$\label{eq:family-regular-level}
 \nabla_{S^{m+1}}\psi_v=N'-bY_{v,b},\qquad
 |\nabla_{S^{m+1}}\psi_v|=r>0
 \quad\hbox{on }\Lambda_{v,b}.$$ Thus $\Lambda_{v,b}=\partial E_{v,b}=\partial^*E_{v,b}$ and its boundary cycle has multiplicity one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-family-central-tube"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-family-central-tube</summary>

*Proof.* Use a tubular neighborhood of the smooth cone on a compact annulus containing $\{sx:x\in\Sigma,\ 1/2\le s\le3/2\}$, constructed inside a slightly larger annulus. Compact embeddedness supplies a uniform $\epsilon>0$ so that, for these central radii and $|h|<\epsilon$, the point $sx+hN(x)$ has unique closest cone point $sx$, and signed distance $h$. This is a tube for the whole cone: after shrinking $\epsilon$, points at radii outside the larger annulus cannot be closest, and uniqueness within that annulus follows from its tubular neighborhood.

For any actual level point $y\in\Lambda_{v,b}$, the boost gives $$|h|=|Cb+S\langle a,y\rangle|\le C|b|+S\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{as }(v,b)\to(0,0).$$ Every closest cone point has radius $r=\sqrt{1-b^2}\to1$. Thus all actual contacts lie in this one fixed tube, so each is unique. Conversely, for every source point $x$, the inverse formula gives $$|h_{v,b}(x)|\le\frac{|b|+Sr}{C-S}\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly on $\Sigma$. Consequently $z=rx+h_{v,b}(x)N(x)$ lies in the same tube and really has signed distance $h_{v,b}(x)$. Its transformed point $Y_{v,b}(x)$ is therefore an actual level point. This proves equality with the entire level and uniqueness of its source representation, not merely existence of one nearby component.

For these parameters the matrix $rI-h_{v,b}A$ is uniformly positive definite, so <a href="#eq:family-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-jacobian">[eq:family-jacobian]</a> gives an immersion. Uniqueness of the contact and invertibility of the boost give injectivity; compactness then gives an embedding. All the formulas are smooth in $(v,b,x)$ near the center.

In the tube $d\varphi=N\cdot dz$. Differentiating the graph relation at one of the level points yields $$0=dh-N\cdot dz
  =D\bigl(db-N'\cdot dy\bigr).$$ The derivative with respect to $b$ is $D>0$, so the graph is smooth near that point and its Euclidean gradient is $N'$. Projecting onto the tangent space of the unit sphere and using <a href="#eq:family-orthogonal-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-orthogonal-decomposition">[eq:family-orthogonal-decomposition]</a> proves <a href="#eq:family-regular-level" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-regular-level">[eq:family-regular-level]</a>. Each level point therefore has a neighborhood in which $E_{v,b}$ is one side of a regular smooth hypersurface. This proves the claimed topological and reduced boundary identities, with multiplicity one. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The extra index direction removes the maximum

<a id="eq:family-positive-form"></a>
Expand the central embedding in the Euclidean parameter $u=(v,b)\in\mathbb R^p$: <a id="eq:family-first-variation"></a>$$\label{eq:family-first-variation}
 Y_{v,b}(x)=x+(b+\langle v,x\rangle)N(x)+O(|u|^2).$$ The error is smooth and uniform with its source derivatives. The functions in <a href="#eq:family-normal-variations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-normal-variations">[eq:family-normal-variations]</a> are independent: integrating a relation $t+\langle d,x\rangle=0$ gives $t=0$, and fullness then gives $d=0$. To evaluate their second variation, use $L=\Delta+m+P$, $P=|A|^2$, and write $w=t+f$, $f=\langle d,x\rangle$. Since $\Delta f=-mf$ and $\int_\Sigma f\,d\mu=0$, $$\label{eq:family-positive-form}
 \int_\Sigma wLw\,d\mu
   =m|\Sigma|t^2+\int_\Sigma P(t+f)^2\,d\mu>0
 \qquad\text{if }(d,t)\ne0.$$ For $t\ne0$ the first term suffices. For $t=0$, $d\ne0$, the nontrivial analytic function $f$ cannot vanish throughout the nonempty open set where $P>0$. The minimal second-variation formula is $\delta^2\mathop{\mathrm{Area}}(w,w)=-\int_\Sigma wLw\,d\mu$. Lemma <a href="#lem:family-central-tube" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-central-tube">24</a> ensures that it computes the mass of the actual boundary family, proving part <span class="upright">(iii)</span> of the proposition.

Assume now that $\mathop{\mathrm{ind}}(\Sigma)>p$. Let $$W=\{t+\langle d,x\rangle:t\in\mathbb R,\ d\in\mathbb R^{m+2}\},
 \qquad
 \mathcal B(\phi,\chi)=\int_\Sigma\phi L\chi\,d\mu.$$ Choose a finite-dimensional subspace spanned by $L$-eigenfunctions with positive eigenvalues, of dimension greater than $p$. The $p$ linear conditions $\mathcal B(\eta,w)=0$ for $w\in W$ admit a nonzero smooth solution $\eta$ in that subspace. Then $\mathcal B(\eta,\eta)>0$, and <a href="#eq:family-positive-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-positive-form">[eq:family-positive-form]</a> implies that $\mathcal B$ is positive definite on $W\oplus\mathbb R\eta$.

<a id="eq:family-morse-estimate"></a>
Extend $\eta N$ to a smooth ambient vector field on $S^{m+1}$, and let $F_s$ be its flow. For small $u,s$, set $$E(u,s)=F_s(E_u),\qquad
 \mathcal A(u,s)=\operatorname{Per}(E(u,s)),
 \qquad E_u=E_{v,b}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:family-central-tube" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-central-tube">24</a> makes $\mathcal A$ smooth in a neighborhood of $(0,0)$. Its first derivative there vanishes by minimality, and its Hessian is negative definite, by the preceding positive-form calculation. Taylor’s theorem therefore gives constants $c>0$ and $\delta>0$ such that $$\label{eq:family-morse-estimate}
 \mathcal A(u,s)\le|\Sigma|-c(|u|^2+s^2)
 \qquad\text{when }|u|^2+s^2\le\delta^2.$$ Shrink $\delta$ so that this ball lies inside the central smooth parameter region.

Choose a smooth nonnegative function $\beta(u)$ supported in $|u|<\delta/2$ and equal to one near $u=0$. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, replace $E_u$ on that region by $E(u,\varepsilon\beta(u))$ and leave it unchanged elsewhere. The compact graph $$\{(u,\varepsilon\beta(u)):|u|\le\delta/2\}$$ misses $(0,0)$. It is contained in the ball on which <a href="#eq:family-morse-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-morse-estimate">[eq:family-morse-estimate]</a> holds, so its area maximum is strictly below $|\Sigma|$. On the complement of $|u|<\delta/2$, the same strict upper bound, possibly with a different positive gap, follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:family-strict-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-strict-loss">23</a>. The boundaries of the modified domains consequently define a map $\widetilde\Phi$ satisfying <a href="#eq:family-strict-deformation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-strict-deformation">[eq:family-strict-deformation]</a>.

Replacing $\varepsilon$ by $t\varepsilon$, $0\le t\le1$, gives a flat homotopy, fixed outside the chosen interior neighborhood of $Q$. It descends through the quotient and preserves the detecting class. On the modified region, the boundaries belong to a compact smooth family of embeddings of $\Sigma$, and hence have no concentration of mass. For completeness, a finite cover by coordinate graph patches, with uniform metric and first-derivative bounds over the compact parameter set, bounds their masses in radius-$\rho$ balls by a constant times $\rho^m$. Outside that region the original no-concentration estimate applies unchanged. This proves part <span class="upright">(iv)</span>. Part <span class="upright">(ii)</span> is Lemma <a href="#lem:family-strict-loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:family-strict-loss">23</a>, so the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:lorentz-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lorentz-family">18</a> is complete.

<a id="sec:cones"></a>
### Low-density cones and attainment

We now obtain the compactness needed to minimize among counterexamples. The regularity argument has two induction parameters: the hypersurface dimension $m$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:volume">1</a>, and the dimension of a Euclidean cone. Keeping them separate is essential. The theorem in dimensions less than $m$ will classify cones of dimension at most $m$, and these classifications will give a smooth link for a cone of dimension $m+1$.

For a varifold $V$, its weight measure is $\|V\|$ and its total mass, when finite, is $\mathbf M(V)$. For a smooth embedded submanifold $S$, write $\mathbf v(S)$ for its associated varifold with multiplicity one. For an integral $k$-varifold in Euclidean space, $[V]$ denotes the locally rectifiable flat chain with coefficients in $\mathbb Z_2$ obtained by reducing its integer multiplicity modulo two. We call $V$ *cyclic modulo two* if $\partial[V]=0$ locally in the ambient open set. This convention applies to noncompact cones and their translates, using locally flat chains as in (White 2009, sec. 2.1 and Appendix) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[White2009](#ref-White2009)].

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Coning, density, and the radial factor

<a id="stmt-lem-cone-operation"></a>
<div id="lem:cone-operation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cones-mass"></a>
**Lemma 25** (The cone operation). *Let $d\geq1$ and let $V$ be a stationary integral $d$-varifold in the unit sphere $S^{d+1}$. Its Euclidean cone $\mathcal C(V)$ is a stationary integral $(d+1)$-varifold in $\mathbb R^{d+2}$, and $$\label{eq:cones-mass}
 \|\mathcal C(V)\|(B_R(0))
   =\frac{R^{d+1}}{d+1}\mathbf M(V),
 \qquad
 \Theta(\mathcal C(V),0)=\frac{\mathbf M(V)}{\sigma_d}.$$ If $V$ is a finite sum of smooth closed embedded minimal hypersurfaces with positive integer multiplicities, then $\mathcal C(V)$ is cyclic modulo two.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cone-operation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cone-operation</summary>

*Proof.* At a spherical point $x$ with tangent plane $P\subset x^\perp$, assign to $rx$ the plane $\mathbb Rx\oplus P$ and the measure $r^d\,dr\,dV(x,P)$. This defines the cone and gives <a href="#eq:cones-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-mass">[eq:cones-mass]</a>. The polar map is a diffeomorphism away from the origin; rectifiability and integer multiplicity there follow from the area formula. The origin has zero mass.

For stationarity, first take a smooth ambient field $X$ supported away from the origin and write $X(rx)=f(r,x)x+Y_r(x)$, where $Y_r(x)\perp x$. The trace of its derivative on the cone plane is $$\operatorname{div}_{\mathbb Rx\oplus P}X(rx)
 =\partial_r f(r,x)+\frac{d}{r}f(r,x)
       +\frac1r\operatorname{div}^{S}_{P}Y_r(x).$$ After multiplication by $r^d$ and integration, the first two terms integrate to the endpoint values of $r^df$, and the last term vanishes by spherical stationarity for each $r$. Thus the cone is stationary off the origin. For a general compactly supported $X$, insert a radial cutoff that is zero on $B_\varepsilon$ and one outside $B_{2\varepsilon}$, with derivative bounded by $c/\varepsilon$. Formula <a href="#eq:cones-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-mass">[eq:cones-mass]</a> bounds the omitted first-variation terms by $c_X(\varepsilon^{d+1}+\varepsilon^d)$, which tends to zero.

For the last assertion, $[\mathcal C(V)]$ has zero boundary on every annulus: an odd component multiplicity gives a smooth mod-two product cylinder, and an even multiplicity gives zero. Truncate this chain inside radius $\varepsilon$. Its difference from the full chain has mass at most $\mathbf M(V)\varepsilon^{d+1}/(d+1)$, whereas its inner boundary has mass at most $\mathbf M(V)\varepsilon^d$. The truncated chains therefore converge locally flat to $[\mathcal C(V)]$ and their boundaries converge locally flat to zero. Continuity of the boundary operator proves $\partial[\mathcal C(V)]=0$ also at the vertex. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:cones-white"></a>
We use the stationary monotonicity formula, tangent-cone existence, and integral-varifold closure of Allard (Allard 1972, sec. 5 and Theorem 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[Allard1972](#ref-Allard1972)]; see also the formulations in (Simon 2018, chap. 4, §3) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[SimonGMT](#ref-SimonGMT)] and (White 2009, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[White2009](#ref-White2009)]. In particular, on a fixed open set, locally bounded mass and locally bounded first variation give a subsequence with an integral-varifold limit. For the stationary sequences below the first variation is identically zero. White’s convergence theorem then gives the additional implication $$\label{eq:cones-white}
 V_i\longrightarrow V,\quad \delta V_i=0,\quad
 \partial[V_i]=0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \partial[V]=0,$$ provided the masses are locally bounded. Indeed, Allard gives integrality of $V$; the first variations satisfy White’s local bound with bound zero; and the boundary chains converge to zero. These are precisely the hypotheses of (White 2009, Definition 3.1 and Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[White2009](#ref-White2009)]. The same statement applies after any translations and dilations, and hence to the blow-ups used below.

<a id="stmt-lem-cones-density"></a>
<div id="lem:cones-density" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cones-density-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Density at every support point). *Let $V$ be a stationary integral $k$-varifold in an open set $U\subset\mathbb R^n$. At every $z\in\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\|V\|$, $\Theta(V,z)\geq1$. If $V=\mathcal C$ is a cone in all of $\mathbb R^n$, then $$\label{eq:cones-density-comparison}
 1\leq\Theta(\mathcal C,z)\leq\Theta(\mathcal C,0)
 \qquad (z\in\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\|\mathcal C\|).$$ Every tangent cone at such a point is nonzero and has density $\Theta(\mathcal C,z)$ at its vertex.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cones-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cones-density</summary>

*Proof.* Integrality gives density at least one at $\|V\|$-almost every point. Every neighborhood of $z$ has positive mass, so choose such points $x_i\to z$. If $\overline{B_r(z)}\subset U$ and $d_i=|x_i-z|<r$, monotonicity at $x_i$ and ball inclusion give $$\|V\|(B_r(z))\geq\|V\|(B_{r-d_i}(x_i))
       \geq\omega_k(r-d_i)^k.$$ Let $i\to\infty$, then $r\downarrow0$. This proves the asserted lower bound also at support points that are exceptional for rectifiability.

For a cone, its homogeneous mass measure gives $\|\mathcal C\|(B_R(0))=\omega_k\Theta(\mathcal C,0)R^k$. For $R>|z|$, the inclusions $$B_{R-|z|}(0)\subset B_R(z)\subset B_{R+|z|}(0)$$ identify the limit of the mass ratios centered at $z$ as $R\to\infty$ with $\Theta(\mathcal C,0)$. Monotonicity proves the upper bound in <a href="#eq:cones-density-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-density-comparison">[eq:cones-density-comparison]</a>. Tangent-cone existence and the monotonicity formula give the stated tangent density, which is positive by the first part. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-cones-splitting"></a>
<div id="lem:cones-splitting" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cones-factor-density"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Splitting the radial line). *Let $k\geq2$ and let $\mathcal C$ be a stationary integral $k$-dimensional cone in $\mathbb R^{k+1}$. For $0\ne z\in\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\|\mathcal C\|$, every tangent cone $T$ at $z$ has the form $$T=\mathbf v(\mathbb Re)\times W,\qquad e=z/|z|,$$ where $W$ is a nonzero stationary integral $(k-1)$-dimensional cone in $e^\perp\cong\mathbb R^k$ and $$\label{eq:cones-factor-density}
 \Theta(W,0)=\Theta(T,0)=\Theta(\mathcal C,z).$$ If $\mathcal C$ is cyclic modulo two, so are $T$ and $W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cones-splitting"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cones-splitting</summary>

*Proof.* Write $T_i=(\eta_{z,\rho_i})_\#\mathcal C\to T$, where $\eta_{z,\rho_i}(y)=(y-z)/\rho_i$ and $\rho_i\downarrow0$. For fixed $b\in\mathbb R$, dilation invariance of $\mathcal C$ by the positive factors $1+b\rho_i$ implies invariance of $T_i$ under $$y\longmapsto(1+b\rho_i)y+bz.$$ Passing to the limit shows that $T$ is invariant under translation by $bz$. Its weight consequently disintegrates as $\|T\|=\mathcal L^1\otimes\nu$ on $\mathbb Re\oplus e^\perp$.

We check that this is an integral varifold product. At almost every rectifiable point of $T$, a measure blow-up is an integer multiple of the measure on its approximate tangent plane. These blow-ups retain invariance under translations in the $e$-direction, so that plane contains $e$. The tangential Jacobian of the coordinate $\pi(te+w)=t$ is therefore one almost everywhere. Rectifiable coarea gives, for almost every $t$, a $(k-1)$-rectifiable slice with the original integer multiplicities; its tangent plane is the intersection of the tangent plane of $T$ with $e^\perp$. Product disintegration identifies the weight of this slice, after translation to $e^\perp$, with $\nu$. Thus $\nu$ defines an integral varifold $W$ and the tangent-plane identification gives $T=\mathbf v(\mathbb Re)\times W$ as varifolds. Here we use rectifiable coarea (Federer 1978, sec. 3, p. 300) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[Federer1978](#ref-Federer1978)].

For a compactly supported field $Y$ on $e^\perp$ and a compactly supported smooth function $\chi$ on $\mathbb R$ with $\int\chi\ne0$, test stationarity of $T$ with $X(te+w)=\chi(t)Y(w)$. Since $Y$ is perpendicular to $e$, $$0=\delta T(X)=\left(\int_{\mathbb R}\chi(t)\,dt\right)\delta W(Y).$$ Hence $W$ is stationary. Dilation invariance of the tangent cone $T$ and the product formula imply dilation invariance of $W$. Writing $\theta=\Theta(W,0)$, integration over a ball gives $$\|T\|(B_1)
 =\theta\omega_{k-1}
       \int_{-1}^1(1-t^2)^{(k-1)/2}\,dt
 =\theta\omega_k.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:cones-factor-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-factor-density">[eq:cones-factor-density]</a>; Lemma <a href="#lem:cones-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cones-density">26</a> makes $W$ nonzero.

If $\partial[\mathcal C]=0$, every $T_i$ is cyclic, and <a href="#eq:cones-white" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-white">[eq:cones-white]</a> implies $\partial[T]=0$. The mod-two slicing identity for the boundary (White 1999, sec. 3, equation (1) and Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[White1999](#ref-White1999)] gives $$\partial\langle[T],\pi,t\rangle
       =\langle\partial[T],\pi,t\rangle=0
 \quad\text{for almost every }t,$$ where signs disappear modulo two. For the locally flat chain, apply the identity on a countable exhaustion by relatively compact regions; restriction compatibility gives one full-measure set of levels where it holds locally everywhere. Choose one such level where the preceding rectifiable slice description also holds. The slice there is the translate of $[W]$ to $te+e^\perp$, because $T$ is the product just established. Translation back proves $\partial[W]=0$. No assertion about whether zero is a good slicing level is needed. ◻

</details>

For clarity, we also record the precise regularity consequence used in the dimension reduction.

<a id="stmt-lem-cones-allard"></a>
<div id="lem:cones-allard" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (A multiplicity-one planar tangent). *Let $V$ be a stationary integral $k$-varifold, $k\geq2$, in an open subset of Euclidean space. If one tangent cone at $z$ is a multiplicity-one plane, then in a neighborhood of $z$, $V$ is a smooth embedded minimal submanifold with multiplicity one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cones-allard"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cones-allard</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Allard’s interior regularity theorem (Allard 1972, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[Allard1972](#ref-Allard1972)], in the formulation of (Simon 2018, chap. 5, hypotheses (5.1) and Theorem 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[SimonGMT](#ref-SimonGMT)], on a sufficiently small rescaled ball from the sequence giving that tangent. Its hypotheses hold as follows. Integrality supplies the almost-everywhere density lower bound one. Stationarity supplies zero generalized mean curvature and zero first variation, hence the required mean-curvature bound for any exponent greater than $k$. Convergence to the multiplicity-one plane supplies normalized mass arbitrarily close to $\omega_k$ and arbitrarily small height and tangent-plane excess on a fixed smaller ball. The center remains in the support. Allard’s theorem therefore gives a single $C^{1,\alpha}$ graph near the center. On that connected graph the stationary integral multiplicity is constant; its tangent multiplicity is one, so the constant is one. The higher-regularity conclusion of (Simon 2018, chap. 5, Theorem 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[SimonGMT](#ref-SimonGMT)], applied with the smoothly prescribed mean curvature identically zero, makes the graph smooth; that statement permits arbitrary codimension. This theorem has no stability hypothesis and no upper bound on $k$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The cone induction

<a id="stmt-prop-low-density-cones"></a>
<div id="prop:low-density-cones" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:cones-threshold"></a>
**Proposition 29** (Low-density cones). *Fix $m\geq2$ and assume Theorem <a href="#thm:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:volume">1</a> in dimensions $2,\ldots,m-1$, with an empty assumption when $m=2$. Let $\mathcal C$ be a nonzero stationary integral $k$-dimensional cone in $\mathbb R^{k+1}$ such that $$\label{eq:cones-threshold}
 \Theta(\mathcal C,0)<\vartheta_m.$$ When $m=2$ or $m=3$, also assume that $\mathcal C$ is cyclic modulo two. Then:*

1.  *if $1\leq k\leq m$, $\mathcal C$ is a multiplicity-one hyperplane;*

2.  *if $k=m+1$, $\mathcal C$ is smooth embedded with multiplicity one away from its vertex. Its link in $S^{m+1}$ is a smooth closed connected embedded minimal hypersurface.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-low-density-cones"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-low-density-cones</summary>

*Proof.* Hold $m$ fixed throughout. We prove (i) by induction in $k$ and then obtain (ii) from the final induction step.

An integral one-dimensional cone is a finite union of rays with positive integer weights: radial slicing of its conical rectifiable measure gives a zero-dimensional integral measure on the unit circle, and local finiteness makes its total weight finite. Write the distinct unit ray directions as $u_j$, their weights as $q_j$, and $N=\sum_jq_j$. Stationarity at the origin says $$\sum_jq_ju_j=0,\qquad \Theta(\mathcal C,0)=N/2.$$ If $m\geq4$, Lemma <a href="#lem:clifford-ratios" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:clifford-ratios">2</a> gives $\vartheta_m\leq3/2$, so the strict bound <a href="#eq:cones-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-threshold">[eq:cones-threshold]</a> gives $N<3$. Nonzero stationarity excludes $N=1$, leaving two opposite rays of weight one. If $m=2$ or $3$, the same lemma gives $\vartheta_m<2$, hence $N<4$; cyclicity says that the boundary coefficient $N$ at the origin is even. Again $N=2$ and balance gives a multiplicity-one line. This proves the base case in both branches.

Suppose next that $2\leq k\leq m+1$ and that (i) has been proved in dimension $k-1$. At any nonzero support point $z$, Lemma <a href="#lem:cones-splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cones-splitting">27</a> expresses a tangent as $\mathbf v(\mathbb R)\times W$. Its factor is nonzero, stationary and integral, is cyclic when required, and has density at most $\Theta(\mathcal C,0)<\vartheta_m$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:cones-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cones-density">26</a>. The induction hypothesis makes $W$ a multiplicity-one plane. Thus this tangent of $\mathcal C$ is a multiplicity-one plane, and Lemma <a href="#lem:cones-allard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cones-allard">28</a> proves smooth embedded multiplicity-one structure near $z$.

It follows that $\Lambda=\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\|\mathcal C\|\cap S^k$ is a smooth closed embedded $(k-1)$-manifold. The radial direction lies in the cone tangent plane and is normal to the sphere, so this intersection is transverse. Polar coarea gives $\mathcal C=\mathcal C(\mathbf v(\Lambda))$ with multiplicity one. Reversing the angular first-variation calculation in Lemma <a href="#lem:cone-operation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cone-operation">25</a> shows that $\Lambda$ is minimal in $S^k$.

Each connected component $\Lambda_j$ is compact and minimal, and its cone is stationary. Lemma <a href="#lem:cones-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cones-density">26</a> applied to that cone, together with <a href="#eq:cones-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-mass">[eq:cones-mass]</a>, yields <a id="eq:cones-component"></a>$$\label{eq:cones-component}
 \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(\Lambda_j)}{\sigma_{k-1}}\geq1.$$ Since the total cone density is less than $\vartheta_m<2$, there is only one component.

If $k=2$, a closed connected embedded minimal curve in $S^2$ is a great circle by the geodesic equation, so its cone is a plane. If $3\leq k\leq m$ and $\Lambda$ were not totally geodesic, the outer induction in dimension $k-1$ would give $$\Theta(\mathcal C,0)
   =\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(\Lambda)}{\sigma_{k-1}}
   \geq\vartheta_{k-1}\geq\vartheta_m,$$ contrary to <a href="#eq:cones-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-threshold">[eq:cones-threshold]</a>. Thus $\Lambda$ is a great sphere and $\mathcal C$ a multiplicity-one plane, completing (i). For $k=m+1$, the already proved plane assertion in dimension $m$ gives exactly the smoothness and connectedness asserted in (ii). We do not apply the dimension-$m$ volume statement to this last link. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-cones-spherical-output"></a>
<div id="cor:cones-spherical-output" class="corollary">

**Corollary 30** (Spherical stationary output). *Under the outer induction hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:low-density-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:low-density-cones">29</a>, let $V\ne0$ be a stationary integral $m$-varifold in $S^{m+1}$ with $\mathbf M(V)<a_m$. If $m=2$ or $3$, assume additionally that $\mathcal C(V)$ is cyclic modulo two; this holds, in particular, if $V$ has smooth closed embedded support with integer component multiplicities. Then $V=\mathbf v(\Sigma)$ for a smooth closed connected embedded minimal hypersurface $\Sigma$. If $\mathbf M(V)>\sigma_m$, this hypersurface is not an equator.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-cones-spherical-output"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-cones-spherical-output</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:cone-operation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cone-operation">25</a> and the mass bound put $\mathcal C(V)$ under part (ii) of Proposition <a href="#prop:low-density-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:low-density-cones">29</a>. Its smooth multiplicity-one link identifies $V$ by the polar cone formula. An equator has area $\sigma_m$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Exact attainment

<a id="stmt-prop-attainment"></a>
<div id="prop:attainment" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:cones-attainment-gap"></a>
**Proposition 31** (A least counterexample). *Fix $m\geq2$ and assume Theorem <a href="#thm:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:volume">1</a> in dimensions $2,\ldots,m-1$. Suppose the class $\mathcal E_m$ of smooth closed connected embedded minimal hypersurfaces $\Sigma\subset S^{m+1}$ satisfying $$\Sigma\text{ is not totally geodesic},\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(\Sigma)<a_m$$ is nonempty. Then $$H=\inf_{\Sigma\in\mathcal E_m}\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(\Sigma)$$ is attained by some $\Sigma_*\in\mathcal E_m$, and $$\label{eq:cones-attainment-gap}
 \sigma_m<\gamma_m\leq H<a_m.$$ Here $\gamma_m$ is the detection gap from Lemma <a href="#lem:detection-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detection-gap">14</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-attainment"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-attainment</summary>

*Proof.* For every member of $\mathcal E_m$, the family of Proposition <a href="#prop:lorentz-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lorentz-family">18</a> is detecting and has maximum mass at most its area. Lemma <a href="#lem:detection-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detection-gap">14</a> therefore gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}(\Sigma)\geq\gamma_m>\sigma_m$. Nonemptiness gives $H<a_m$, proving <a href="#eq:cones-attainment-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-attainment-gap">[eq:cones-attainment-gap]</a>.

<a id="eq:cones-exact-sequence"></a>
Choose $\Sigma_i\in\mathcal E_m$ with areas $H_i\to H$ and set $\mathcal C_i=\mathcal C(\mathbf v(\Sigma_i))$. These cones are stationary, integral, and cyclic by Lemma <a href="#lem:cone-operation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cone-operation">25</a>. Their exact masses satisfy $$\label{eq:cones-exact-sequence}
 \|\mathcal C_i\|(B_R(0))=\frac{H_iR^{m+1}}{m+1}
 \qquad(R>0).$$ Consequently their masses are uniformly bounded on every compact set. Allard’s integral-varifold compactness theorem and a diagonal subsequence give a stationary integral limit $\mathcal C$ in all of $\mathbb R^{m+2}$. Every fixed dilation leaves each $\mathcal C_i$ invariant, so by continuity of pushforward it leaves $\mathcal C$ invariant as well. White’s theorem in the form <a href="#eq:cones-white" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-white">[eq:cones-white]</a> gives $\partial[\mathcal C]=0$.

We verify that the limiting mass is exactly $H$ at the spherical level. The weight of the conical limit is homogeneous of degree $m+1$. It has zero mass on every sphere of positive radius: otherwise dilation would produce infinitely many disjoint spheres with uniformly positive mass in a fixed compact annulus. Weak convergence can thus be evaluated on every centered ball. Passing to the limit in <a href="#eq:cones-exact-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cones-exact-sequence">[eq:cones-exact-sequence]</a> yields $$\|\mathcal C\|(B_R(0))=\frac{HR^{m+1}}{m+1},
 \qquad
 \Theta(\mathcal C,0)=\frac H{\sigma_m}<\vartheta_m.$$ In particular $\mathcal C$ is nonzero. This exact identity retains the infimum value; a lower-semicontinuity bound would not give the same conclusion by itself.

Proposition <a href="#prop:low-density-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:low-density-cones">29</a>(ii) now makes the link $\Sigma_*=\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\|\mathcal C\|\cap S^{m+1}$ a smooth closed connected embedded minimal hypersurface, with multiplicity one. Its area is exactly $H$. The bounds $\sigma_m<H<a_m$ exclude an equator and put $\Sigma_*$ in $\mathcal E_m$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Proof of the volume theorem

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-attainment-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-attainment</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:volume">1</a>.* We induct on $m\ge2$. Fix $m$, assuming the theorem in dimensions $2,\ldots,m-1$; this hypothesis is empty when $m=2$. By Proposition <a href="#prop:immersion-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:immersion-reduction">3</a>, every counterexample in dimension $m$ would be a smooth closed connected embedded minimal hypersurface.

Suppose such counterexamples exist. Proposition <a href="#prop:attainment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:attainment">31</a> gives one of least area $H$, denoted by $\Sigma$, with $$\sigma_m<\gamma_m\le H<a_m.$$ Set $p=m+3$. Proposition <a href="#prop:index-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:index-rigidity">10</a> gives $\mathop{\mathrm{ind}}(\Sigma)\ge p$. Equality would make $\Sigma$ a minimal Clifford product and force $H\ge a_m$. Therefore $\mathop{\mathrm{ind}}(\Sigma)>p$.

By Proposition <a href="#prop:lorentz-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lorentz-family">18</a>, there is a detecting family without mass concentration whose supremum mass $D_1$ satisfies $D_1<H$. Theorem <a href="#thm:minmax" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:minmax">16</a> yields a stationary integral $m$-varifold $V$ on $S^{m+1}$ with $$\gamma_m\le\|V\|(S^{m+1})<H<a_m.$$ When $m=2,3$, choose the smooth embedded min–max output provided by that theorem, with its integer component multiplicities. Its Euclidean cone is cyclic modulo two by Lemma <a href="#lem:cone-operation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cone-operation">25</a>. In every higher dimension the cone is stationary and integral, which are the required hypotheses.

Corollary <a href="#cor:cones-spherical-output" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:cones-spherical-output">30</a>, using the outer induction hypothesis, now shows that $V$ is a smooth connected embedded minimal hypersurface of multiplicity one. Its mass exceeds $\sigma_m$, so it is non-equatorial. It is therefore a counterexample with area strictly smaller than $H$, a contradiction.

This proves the embedded assertion in dimension $m$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:immersion-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:immersion-reduction">3</a> gives the full immersion assertion. The induction proves the theorem for every $m\ge2$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two decompositions at a fixed closest-point contact, shown schematically with positive normal coefficients. Lorentz invariance makes the coefficient $r=\\sqrt{1-b^2}$ the same in both planes. The source vector $z$ need not be unit length. On a fixed level, $b$ is constant while $h = h_{v, b}(x)$ can vary. The isometry $O$ and normal $N′$ depend on the source point; the picture does not represent a single ambient rotation.",
      "figure_id": "fig:family-normal-planes",
      "pdf_page": 20,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:family-normal-planes",
          "exact_quote": "The two decompositions at a fixed closest-point contact, shown\nschematically with positive normal coefficients. Lorentz invariance\nmakes the coefficient $r=\\sqrt{1-b^2}$ the same in both planes.\nThe source vector $z$ need not be unit length",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Latex,font=\\small]\n  \\begin{scope}\n    \\node at (1.2,2.9) {Source contact plane};\n    \\draw[->] (0,0) -- (2.7,0) node[right] {$x$};\n    \\draw[->] (0,0) -- (0,2.5) node[left] {$N(x)$};\n    \\draw[dashed] (1.6,0) -- (1.6,1.8);\n    \\draw[thick,->,linkblue] (0,0) -- (1.6,1.8);\n    \\fill (1.6,1.8) circle (1.3pt);\n    \\node[above right] at (1.6,1.8) {$z=rx+hN$};\n    \\node[below] at (0.8,0) {$r$};\n    \\node[right] at (1.6,0.9) {$h$};\n  \\end{scope}\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=7cm]\n    \\node at (1.2,2.9) {Transformed normal plane};\n    \\draw[->] (0,0) -- (2.7,0) node[right] {$Ox$};\n    \\draw[->] (0,0) -- (0,2.5) node[left] {$N'(x)$};\n    \\draw[gray] (2,0) arc[start angle=0,end angle=100,radius=2];\n    \\draw[dashed] (1.6,0) -- (1.6,1.2);\n    \\draw[thick,->,linkblue] (0,0) -- (1.6,1.2);\n    \\fill (1.6,1.2) circle (1.3pt);\n    \\node[above right] at (1.6,1.2) {$Y=rOx+bN'$};\n    \\node[below] at (0.8,0) {$r$};\n    \\node[right] at (1.6,0.6) {$b$};\n    \\node at (2.6,2.1) {$|Y|=1$};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two side-by-side planar diagrams illustrating vector decompositions:\n1. Left diagram titled \"Source contact plane\":\n   - Horizontal axis labeled $x$ with an arrow pointing right.\n   - Vertical axis labeled $N(x)$ with an arrow pointing up.\n   - A vector labeled $z = rx + hN$ extending from the origin into the first quadrant.\n   - A horizontal segment labeled $r$ along the $x$-axis from the origin to the projection point.\n   - A vertical dashed line labeled $h$ connecting the tip of $rx$ to the vector tip $z$.\n2. Right diagram titled \"Transformed normal plane\":\n   - Horizontal axis labeled $Ox$ with an arrow pointing right.\n   - Vertical axis labeled $N'(x)$ with an arrow pointing up.\n   - A circular arc centered at the origin labeled $|Y| = 1$.\n   - A unit vector labeled $Y = rOx + bN'$ extending from the origin to the circular arc.\n   - A horizontal segment labeled $r$ along the $Ox$-axis.\n   - A vertical dashed line labeled $b$ connecting the tip of $rOx$ to the vector tip $Y$."
    }
  ],
